>
> @markbecker5951 I know that ethnicity and skin color are different things; I just simplified things to avoid having my comment deleted.
  
> 
  
> In any case, you understood me; we're talking about human groups originating from different regions of the world, period.
  
> 
  
> "Socioeconomic factors are about 10-30%. The rest is genetics."
  
> 
  
> - I find you quite self-assured to place so little importance on the environment in which a person develops.
  
> 
  
> I don't think I'm wrong in stating that regardless of your group, your color, your IQ... if you were raised in a dysfunctional home, it will negatively influence your behavior.
  
> We know that the environment in which a child develops plays a crucial role in shaping the adult they will become. So the 10-30% figure seems like a huge underestimate to me.
  
> 
  
> Then you're conflating IQ and behavior. You say that IQ is genetic and that even with favorable socioeconomic factors it can be improved, but not significantly. However, IQ measures a form of intelligence; it in no way measures your sociability and what goes with it.
  
> 
  
> Similarly, to answer your question about IQ, I've read studies that claim there are no significant differences between children of different backgrounds if they are raised in the same privileged environments.
  
> 
  
> In short, it's extremely difficult to know the respective roles of genetics and environment in individuals' antisocial behaviors, as these two factors are so omnipresent and co-evolve during development.
  
> 
  
> That's why I maintain that, rather than relying on ethnic or physical stereotypes, it would be more relevant (even if we do use stereotypes) to focus on attitudes and indicators of social class. To make generalizations, not to judge individually.

### **@Herzquelle** | 👍 2231 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hE4l9WyLF3U&lc=UgzGHjFKd6A_y5_irlp4AaABAg)

This lecture is right that game theory matters. Players, rules, and incentives explain behavior better than morals or ideology. But what’s missing is that once a game runs long enough, the incentives stop being abstract. They rewire biology, psychology, and daily stress until behavior becomes automatic.

In modern societies the main incentive is money and status, not because people are greedy, but because money became the universal access token to safety. Housing, healthcare, education, dignity, time, and future options all go through markets. Communal buffers are gone. So the real game is not “get rich” but “don’t fall.” When inequality is high, falling isn’t a step down, it’s a cliff. Biologically, that reads as threat.
Constant threat means chronic stress. Cortisol stays high, dopamine systems shift toward short-term validation, novelty, and comparison. Long-term bonding weakens, fertility hormones drop. People delay commitment and children, not because they hate family, but because under stress the human system postpones reproduction. Psychology then rationalizes it as preference, but the driver is regulation.
Status today means position in a hierarchy that decides how safe your life is. Algorithms force endless upward social comparison. Richer, prettier, more successful lives are always visible. Status becomes zero-sum and unstable. “Good enough” stops feeling safe. So in the dating game people don’t optimize for love, they optimize against risk. This is why the Nash equilibrium model breaks in real life. Real societies are not clean markets, they are manipulated status arenas.

Poor countries don’t hit this wall yet because the game is different. Survival is collective. Extended family buffers risk. Children add resilience instead of vulnerability. Comparison is local, not global. Reproduction still makes biological sense there, even under hardship.
Now here’s the part history keeps screaming and everyone ignores. Systems like this always overproduce elites. Too many credentialed aspirants, too many status seekers, too few real positions. The middle buffer gets squeezed. Downward mobility fear explodes. Elite competition intensifies while the productive base erodes. Rome did this when smallholders disappeared. Other empires did it when wealth concentrated and titles outpaced real capacity. The pattern is old.

Elite overproduction plus inequality turns society into a pressure cooker. Everyone competes upward. Nobody wants to reproduce downward. Fertility collapses. Community dissolves. Stress becomes permanent. Consumerism and platforms profit because they monetize anxiety and comparison. Asset holders benefit because precarity keeps people compliant. Governments try bonuses, but bonuses don’t change the rules of the game.

This isn’t men vs. women or morals failing. It’s structure. When safety is tied to hierarchy and status, and the system overproduces elites while hollowing out the middle, the outcome is always the same. Humans adapt perfectly. The civilization doesn’t.
Game theory explains the mechanics exactly as the lecture says: players, rules, incentives. But unless you change the incentives and stop elite overproduction, the game always ends the same way. The middle collapses, fertility drops, the dependency ratio explodes, and internal competition intensifies. The state tries to compensate with debt, coercion, or narrative control, while elites turn on each other as upward mobility stalls. Eventually pressure has to be released. Historically that release comes through external conflict or internal violence. War resets hierarchies, destroys excess capital, absorbs surplus elites, and reasserts control over populations. If war is not possible, the system drifts into stagnation and decay until fragmentation or collapse follows. Not because people are stupid, but because the structure trains everyone to optimize short-term survival while liquidating the future.

> @cybill8816 Used ChatGPT to condense, i asked myself the same question months ago, here is what i found as solutions from different books, history, and the feedback loops of mankinds behaviour :  
> Elite overproduction isn’t a moral or cultural problem, it’s structural. The system keeps producing too many people who are forced to aim for elite positions because basic safety at the middle has disappeared. Credentials, banks, housing, and money creation all reinforce the same loop.  
>   
> Bachelor’s and master’s degrees stopped being education and became gatekeepers. They’re used as filters for jobs that don’t need them, creating artificial scarcity and manufacturing “elite candidates” by the millions. People are pushed into debt, delayed adulthood, and status tracks just to access normal work. That floods the system with surplus aspirants. De-credential jobs, expand paid apprenticeships, and use skill-based hiring instead. The moment degrees stop signaling safety, elite pressure drops.  
>   
> Paper money and credit creation are the silent accelerant. When money is created faster than real productive capacity, it flows into assets, not wages. Housing, stocks, and credentials inflate while everyday work lags behind. Standing still becomes punishment, so everyone is forced into constant competition just to preserve position. People don’t chase money because they love it, they chase it because inflation makes stability impossible.  
>   
> Banks amplify this by directing credit into asset inflation instead of real capacity. Housing and financial markets absorb liquidity, concentrate wealth, and shrink the middle. Safety turns into a bidding war. Change credit rules, limit speculative leverage, and force capital into production and infrastructure. When credit stops inflating status assets, elite overproduction slows automatically.  
>   
> Housing is the pressure multiplier. When shelter is treated as a speculative asset, survival becomes positional. You must outbid others to exist. That alone forces people into elite chasing behavior. Build massively, break local vetoes, and stop treating housing as a wealth store. Make housing boring again and a huge part of the status race disappears.  
>   
> Community used to absorb risk. Family, local networks, shared childcare, predictable lives made failure survivable. That buffer is gone, so risk is individual and people compensate by climbing hierarchies. Rebuild local infrastructure, stable work patterns, and real third places so survival doesn’t depend on rank.  
>   
> Time Windows:  
> In 1933/34 the US broke the domestic gold link, confiscated gold, then revalued it upward. That devalued the dollar overnight, reshuffled balance sheets, reduced debt pressure, and changed incentives fast enough to stabilize the system. It wasn’t fair or clean, but it forced a reset without war. The key wasn’t gold itself, it was breaking a frozen balance-sheet structure that couldn’t resolve internally.  
>   
> China matters now for the same reason. It sits on massive in-house debt tied to property, local governments, and banks. Slow reform won’t clear it. If China ever forces a reserve, currency, or asset repricing to reset that debt, the global financial fallout would be huge. (Chinas hidden Gold Reserve could be up to 30.000 Tons) Silver prices will explode maybe thats why they banned the export. But that shock could open a narrow non-war window where old rules stop functioning and structural reform becomes unavoidable.  
> Historically pressure is released either through war or through a hard financial reset that breaks incentives quickly. Climate stress narrows the window further by increasing instability. If the reset doesn’t happen in time, history usually chooses the violent path, revolution, civil war, external wars.  
>   
> So stopping elite overproduction isn’t about changing culture or blaming individuals. It’s about structure. Fix credential gatekeeping, stop paper money from inflating status assets, redirect credit, deflate housing, and rebuild community buffers. Do it fast enough during a shock window, or the system will resolve it the old way. And if those structures are not touched, the cycle simply repeats. Elites change, names change, flags change, but the pattern stays the same. The system resets through crisis, new elites take over, and the same incentive structure is rebuilt because it protects those at the top. That is why this loop has repeated for more than two thousand years. Without structural change, collapse is not an end, it is just a handover for the ones who claim power at that time.

> @_1_9 I disagree with the assumption behind that criticism. Originality doesn’t mean isolation, it means seeing patterns and keeping them consistent across domains. Just to be clear, this isn’t one vague idea or a single angle either. I’m working on three connected but distinct things. One is theoretical physics, where I’m developing a relational model of gravity and time that doesn’t rely on dark matter or dark energy, but on how structures stabilize, stiffen, and renew across scales. Alongside that, I’m writing a short essay on ethics, grounded in the idea that harm and order are relational, about preserving coherence and regenerative capacity rather than enforcing control. And I’m also writing a broader book that traces recurring patterns from hunter-gatherer societies to modern systems, using biology as the ground layer and building upward through psychology, economics, technology, and culture, structured through seven interacting clusters. Across all of this, time isn’t something that simply flows, it’s what we perceive when relations are stable enough to carry memory. Gravity and cosmic acceleration aren’t effects of unseen substances, but emergent responses of how structures hold, block, and renew. Measurement, whether in physics or human perception, acts as a filter that stabilizes a slice of reality, which is why observations can contradict without reality itself being inconsistent. The same renewal versus rigidity dynamic shows up in quantum systems, biological stress, social hierarchies, and even ethical behavior. These ideas weren’t copied from one place or another. They come from observing my own brain, my inner self and how the same structure repeats in the world around me, then checking where history, science, and philosophy already noticed parts of it and where they didn’t. That’s not dependence on aggregated knowledge, it’s how you avoid mistaking new words for new understanding.

> @Julia-l2s8w You’re asking the right question. War is not inevitable, but pressure release is when structural reforms are delayed too long. Systems don’t collapse because people are stupid or immoral, they collapse because feedback loops stop working. When that happens, tension must be released, either internally or externally.  
>   
> In the U.S., collapse without war is very possible and historically common. It usually looks like internal fragmentation: loss of institutional legitimacy, elite infighting, financial crises, asset inflation hollowing out the median, rising debt, social unrest, and eventually authoritarian drift or forced redistribution. Think late Roman Republic dynamics, not immediate civil war.  
> Sadly we now have the time where external war is often used to postpone internal correction, but it usually makes the eventual reckoning worse. That’s why decline to “second power” feels unacceptable for the U.S. Empires rarely accept relative decline voluntarily. Europe can absorb decline more easily, but because it ignores the same median problem, it risks an even harder internal explosion later.  
>   
> The core issue most people miss is the median. The median is the foundation (Fundament) of any society. Every stable civilization is a pyramid where the first layer of stone has to be wide, connected, and load-bearing. When the top grows larger and more self-referential than the base (elite overproduction), the structure fails at the bottom.  
> Fertility collapse, political polarization, and narrative control are not cultural failures, they are structural signals. At that point the choice is no longer “if” but how pressure is released: managed reform now, or unmanaged internal and external breakdown later. And yes we still have time but the window is closing fast.

> @LiaEA Frist led me define what i perceive as middle class structured around the median: Historically, the median or middle was not a statistical average as it sounds like today, it was the load-bearing foundation of a society. This meant a broad group of people who could reliably reproduce, replace themselves, and carry institutions forward without constant stress and where content to some degree. The middle had stable access to housing, predictable work, time for family, and a clear path to asset accumulation, even if modest without a degree needed. Their role wasn’t to compete upward, but to anchor continuity and the society and communities. Because the middle was secure, elites could exist without constant pressure from below, and the bottom could aspire upward without panic (the 0,1-1% is always another part of society and is above elite). Biologically, this layer kept reproduction normal, bonding strong, and risk-taking measured. When the median is healthy, stress stays local, fertility is sufficient, and political systems remain correctable. When the median loses housing, time, and future security, it stops functioning as a foundation and becomes a buffer zone under compression. Once that happens, the pyramid doesn’t slowly decline, it destabilizes, because no structure can stand if its base is forced into permanent survival mode.  
> Now lets look at Elite Overproduction:  
> Elite overproduction isn’t a moral failure, it’s biology + incentives doing exactly what the system aka our structures train humans and childs to achieve for success and gratitude. Humans optimize for safety, rank, reproduction, and meaning. And “rank” in modern economies isn’t a noble title like in medieval times, it’s your position in society defined by your income–asset–status and social network that decides how safe your life is! Housing access, healthcare quality, time freedom, partner options, and future security. When safety is tied to rank and rank is scarce, people stay in permanent competition mode. If we now add the structural triggers: we mass-produce credentials, degrees, and “elite signals” faster than we produce real stable roles (ownership, decision power, secure careers, meaningful responsibility). That creates too many high-aspiration players chasing too few top slots because its the only viable option to get the girl (if man) or safety (if woman) (both simplified) you need for your base biological programs survival, breeding, meaning/society acknowledgement. The ones who don’t “make it” don’t just relax into the middle, because falling in this system means losing time, family viability, and security, so they keep competing and they squeeze the layer below. Biologically, chronic competition reads as threat: stress stays high, bonding weakens, long-term planning shrinks, and reproduction gets postponed because the body treats unstable conditions as “not safe to breed.” So elite overproduction isn’t just too many educated people, it’s a feedback loop where status scarcity + credential inflation + asset barriers turn normal human survival and mating programs into a pressure cooker that pushes the median down and destabilizes the whole pyramid due to overqualification and a desperate struggle to keep status, they accept worse conditions, play harder politics, and push standards upward (more degrees, more hoops, more “prove yourself”). That pushes the median down: wages get capped, job ladders get steeper, housing gets more competitive, and the middle loses bargaining power. First through status inflation (you need more paper for the same life), then through market crowding (more competitors for the same slots). Stress rises, time disappears, family formation gets delayed, fertility drops, and the whole system becomes even more zero-sum. Elite overproduction doesn’t just sit above the median in the middle, it presses onto it like weight, and the median cracks first, from not increasing in actual inflation of assets to payrises. The solutions is multi layered and needs to adress different structures of society at the same time and are unpopular, thus they only get implemented after a fallout (external pressure then internal pressure) which gets more dangerous with delaying tactics. This is the real risk our democracies of the west are facing atm. [00:01] - Understanding the core question: Why do societies behave the way they do?
[00:34] - The Religious Theory: Human behavior as a struggle between good and evil.
[01:21] - The Biological Theory: Procreation, gene survival, and gender-based investment strategies.
[03:00] - Race and Culture Theory: Historical struggles for dominance between different groups.
[03:43] - Economic Theory: Money and self-interest as the primary drivers of human action.
[04:01] - Liberalism and Enlightenment: History as a steady progress toward rationality and justice.
00:05:01 - Defining Game Theory
[05:01] - Why Game Theory is the superior framework for predicting human and national behavior.
[05:35] - The 3 Pillars of a Game: Players, Rules (Constraints), and Incentives.
[06:18] - How to use these pillars to predict the outcome of any social interaction.
00:07:07 - 3 Major Benefits of Learning Game Theory
[07:20] - Benefit 1: Becoming a better, more thoughtful decision-maker (Personal Growth).
[08:14] - Benefit 2: Analyzing "stupid" world events through a rational lens (Global Understanding).
[09:47] - Benefit 3: Developing predictive powers to gain sovereignty over your own destiny.
00:11:03 - The Dating Game: A Practical Example
[11:16] - Setting the Scene: 5 males vs. 5 females in a hypothetical marriage market.
[11:40] - The Ranking System: Evaluating players based on Genes, Wealth, and Status.
[13:00] - Biological Strategies: Differences between male maximization and female selectivity.
[14:07] - Why individual maximization leads to social collapse.
00:14:42 - Rational Outcomes vs. Cooperation
[14:53] - The Economic Perspective: How seeking "the best" results in getting "the worst."
[16:17] - The Power of Cooperation: How matching creates a perfect social outcome.
[17:05] - Understanding the Nash Equilibrium: Maximizing outcomes for all players.
00:18:09 - Why Real Life is "Suicidal"
[18:09] - The mismatch between theory and reality: Why people choose irrational paths.
[18:53] - Experiment Results: Contrasting minimum marriage requirements for men vs. women.
[20:03] - The "Billionaire Effect": Hyper-competition for top-tier status symbols.
[20:30] - The Rise of Incels: When players give up on a game they cannot win.
00:21:20 - Status vs. Procreation
[21:28] - The True Incentive: Why the modern game is about Status, not sex or children.
[21:45] - Instagram and Social Bragging: The role of vanity in modern dating choices.
00:23:23 - The Role of the "Superstructure"
[23:38] - Definition of Superstructure: Demographics, economics, and religion as game-shapers.
[24:14] - Example 1: Low-population, high-mortality villages and social cohesion.
[25:03] - Example 2: Growing populations and the necessity of arranged marriages.
[25:32] - Example 3: Overpopulated, high-tech, materialistic societies (The Modern Era).
00:27:53 - The Consequences: Decreasing Fertility
[27:53] - The "Dating Game" as a tool for social mobility at the expense of reproduction.
[28:47] - Global Fertility Crisis: Why wealthy, educated women are refusing to have children.
[30:12] - Why government financial incentives fail to solve the "Zero-Sum" status game.
00:30:48 - The Civilization Life Cycle
[30:48] - Historical Patterns: Comparing modern trends to the collapse of the Roman Empire.
[31:18] - Using Game Theory to identify if a civilization is in birth, maturation, or collapse.
00:33:13 - Data Analysis: Global Fertility Maps
[33:25] - Mapping the World: Identifying "Red Zones" (Growth) vs. "Blue Zones" (Collapse).
[34:07] - The Endgame in North America, Europe, and East Asia.
[34:55] - East Asia's Crisis: Why China and South Korea are in demographic "freefall."
00:36:50 - The Israel Outlier Case Study
[37:00] - Correlating GDP per capita with Fertility rates.
[38:05] - Why Israel is the only high-tech, wealthy nation with above-replacement fertility.
[39:19] - The Role of Existential Threat: How patriotism and survival flip the status game.
00:40:48 - The "Zombie Society" Trend
[41:19] - South Korea's Worst-Case Scenario: A society with no children and only retirees.
[42:01] - Economic and Military Implications: The inability to work or fight wars by 2040-2060.
[43:58] - Resource Concentration: The "Samsung" effect on middle-class child-rearing.
00:44:41 - Q&A: Saudi Arabia and Human Capital
[45:15] - Explaining Saudi Arabia's outlier status: Oil wealth and the welfare state.
[46:10] - The importance of Human Capital and innovation vs. natural resource wealth.
[48:34] - Closing Remarks: Using Game Theory as a guide for further research.

I understand the strategic logic behind the example, but I think it could be framed differently. Instead of focusing mainly on birthrates, taxes, and pushing more people into the workforce, shouldn’t the discussion also consider caregiving responsibilities as a social good in themselves?

A society is not sustained only by accumulating capital or increasing labor participation. It is sustained by care, well-being, and dignified living conditions. Maybe the real strategic question is not how to incentivize people to work more, but how policymakers can improve quality of life, stronger labor rights, better childcare systems, fair wages, and social protection, so people can choose freely how to balance work and care.

Reducing everything to productivity and economic output risks ignoring the human dimension of social reproduction.

This is a truth claim about reality that I don’t even know how one can substantiate. But it’s a lot more nuanced than that. You mentioned the elite overproduction in some of your previous lectures that’s far more of a contributing factor. For example, in both Rome and Sparta, the elites consolidated more and more wealth and power into fewer and fewer hands to the point where in Sparta, for instance, the citizens could not afford to send their children to the military schools, and they had a severe manpower shortage. And Rome, for instance, it led to them, recruiting barbarian, mercenaries, or providing material incentives that made the soldiers loyal to the individuals and not the nation itself, which caused all sorts of civil wars, and problems. Again, it’s a lot more nuance than that.

### **@adelhassan7997** | 👍 14 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hE4l9WyLF3U&lc=UgwIvo8Yn2wf6RFpwhN4AaABAg)

A lot of the specifics of the dating game are presented as assertions without sufficient evidence (e.g. his assertion that there is no monogamy in a low-technology village-- that's a bold claim, where is your proof?, or that dating is exclusively about status-- maybe for some people, but not for everyone, and the exceptions matter in game theory), but dating is a very interesting demonstration of the general principles of game theory, so still an interesting watch. Just keep in mind that not everything he says is necessarily true, some of it is more to give an example of how game theory works.

Love his last semester, but this premise is completely off. The assumptions Jiang makes do a lot of the heavy lifting in his arguments. Genes wealth status are pretty strong assumptions about mating behavior. This is why game theory fails often in my opinion. It relies so heavily on assumptions and dismisses scenarios that disprove it. For example: Women #1-5 could absolutely all mate with Man #5, and our bonobo ancestors do exactly this, and I believe for most of human history this is what we did. When children are raised in a tribe, the social behavior is different. Jiang's theory is solely predicated on monogamy, which is a new aspect of humanity since we started farming. As nomads, we were not monogamous as a rule. Also, Jiang ignores that first the "game rules" are that women are looking for LOVE, respect, care. Only when that is abandoned do other things come into play. He attempts to address this with the superstructure idea, but it is also predicated on the assumption that superstructures determine human behavior, whereas the reality is throughout history no matter what structure was in place, women mate for love and respect above all. It also does not answer why so many childfree couples exist right now who COULD procreate but refuse. Refusing to procreate is seen throughout nature whenever the environment is unsupportable to young. Which is what human society is right now. Has nothing to do with status and everything to do with environmental destruction, rise is psychopathy, and oppression.

### **@Sangida_Akter** | 👍 13 likes

I strongly disagree with how you framed the societal collapse. It feels more apocalyptic and dystopian than reality.
The current population shouldn’t be treated as an ideal baseline. Just about 100 years ago, the global population was around 2 billion. Historically, roughly 40% of men passed on their genes compared to about 80% of women, due to wars, famine, disease, and class structures. Large numbers of people never even had the opportunity to reproduce( This is how NATUTE works!)
Both history and nature suggest cyclical dynamics rather than a one time collapse. When food and comfort increase, populations grow rapidly; then resource constraints, conflict, or crises reduce numbers, followed by stabilization. This cycle repeats across ecosystems.
Yes, with 8 billion people, we may face decline due to resource limits or political and social pressures. But decline does not automatically mean total collapse. It is far more plausible that we go through cycles of growth, contraction, and stabilization rather than a final dystopian end state.
As a lecturer, it makes sense to present this as a possible trend or scenario but portraying it as how things are “supposed” to end feels overstated.

> @eat_water That is my point. The structural incentives in any modern, industrial, capitalist society are exactly such that a woman making a rational choice chooses not to have a child or to only have a single child; often later in life when her fertility has already decreased. But the important part is that you can't change that by improving variables such as housing cost, wages, and cost of living in general as you are arguing. A woman in Ethiopia or Nigeria doesn't fare better than a woman in the developed world in any of those metrics, yet she still consistently chooses to have more children. The key thing is that the ambition for higher economic standards of living (status) leads women to spend their most fertile years pursing ways to climb up the economic ladder (education, work, "marring up", etc...) while in less developed countries women tend not to have such economic ambitions and therefore they are content with their lot in life; spending their time instead developing a family. The chase for increased economic status within a capitalist society is inherently opposed to the development of large families.

### **@zadok6709**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hE4l9WyLF3U&lc=UgwPiw5ISvUP-y2kex14AaABAg)

It’s interesting that Prof Jiang highlights how the West has lost love of neighbour in particular. That stood out to me because the second half of the Ten Commandments is exactly about that: How a people relate to one another in a social and communal sense. Take the command to honour your father and mother. At first glance, it seems like a simple personal rule with a promise of longer life. But in its original context, it’s addressed to the whole nation of Israel and tied to remaining in the land. That shifts the meaning: it’s not just about individual lifespan, but about the survival and stability of a people. “Father and mother” can be understood not only as biological parents, but as representing the authority, heritage, and continuity of the nation itself; the generations that came before and the structure that holds society together. Honouring them means respecting that inherited order, not discarding it. Once that breaks down, it’s not just family relationships that suffer, it’s the nation’s continuity. People become detached from their roots, less willing to sacrifice for the common good, and more driven by individual gain or status. Over time, that weakens the entire civilisation. And what we’re seeing today can be read as the consequence of ignoring that. Where the breakdown isn’t just personal but civilisational.

### **@hedonistky**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hE4l9WyLF3U&lc=UgwD60nhn0LtO1xyLGt4AaABAg)

Hello Professor Jiang, first and foremost thank you so much for making your knowledge and wisdom widely accessible to all of us - high-quality knowledge and wisdom is scarce and I really appreciate your selflessness in sharing your knowledge with all of us.

I would like to ask a few clarifying questions, if I may:

1. Could money/material be seen as the simpler, overarching superstructure across all stages of civilization? In all three stages of civilization that you mentioned, it seems like their behavior can all be explained by their desire to maximize goods and services. For example, the women in poor villages who behave as they do to maximize care for their children, the families in growing societies who arrange marriages to maximize economic gains and in wealthy societies who marry up to (again) maximize their statuses which is a proxy for influence that is used again to obtain more goods and services. 

2. You extrapolated the survival of countries based on their fertility rates, which is likely a proxy for the growth of their labor force (as you acknowledged immigration helps). Do you think AI/humanoid robotics would change this calculus, as its success (which is a huge assumption, of course) would change the fundamental constitution of labor forces in the wealthiest countries, allowing the labor forces of the most advanced countries to continue growing without a growth in their human populations?

8:21 Regarding the claim that there was "no good reason" to go after Maduro: This overlooks critical strategic and humanitarian realities. Firstly, the intervention was tied to the global energy supply chain; Venezuela’s extra-heavy crude oil is useless without the specific refining capabilities and diluents often provided by Western infrastructure. Stabilizing this sector was a matter of securing global energy markets.

Secondly, from a humanitarian perspective, the status quo under Maduro had produced the worst economic collapse in the region's history. Millions were pushed into extreme poverty and forced to migrate. For many Venezuelans, US pressure wasn't an "infringement" but a lifeline toward a better economic outlook. To say there was 'no reason' ignores the moral and economic imperative to replace a regime that had presided over hyperinflation and the literal starvation of its population.

Hmm, I'd be interested to see the fertility rates here in Australia over the past 50-odd years.  The slummy suburbs are/were full of folks having armies of children.  But I do remember a government campaign from decades ago pushing the "have one kid for mum, one for dad, and one for the country" idea.

I'd always been against people having too many kids.  "We'd have so many fewer problems if there was 8 million humans rather than 8 billion!"  I guess I never really "understood" the aging/disappearing population concern for a long time.  I get it now though.  I just wish the lesser of us (genetically and social status-wise) didn't procreate.  I include myself in that.  I've got terrible genes, and always had a poor social status and future prospects.. ### **@myporcelainmask** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hE4l9WyLF3U&lc=UgyhNDUn1T4N_BLLh1B4AaABAg)

I understand there wasn't really time to dive into the reasons for declining birth rates as it's not the main point of the video, but saying women have stopped giving birth to chase status is the kind of fundamental misunderstanding that is keeping governments from solving the problem in the first place. Yes, South Korea has failed to raise birth rates despite their many efforts to throw money at the situation, but they're also completely ignoring what the women themselves are saying are the reasons they remain childless. 

Many women want to have kids, but will not until the conditions in which they have to raise their child improve. The extreme misogyny among males leaves women without viable options to procreate with. Women don't want to build a family with someone who hates them and sees them as lesser. Many young Korean men and women remember the stress and pressures of the educational system here and don't want their children to go through the same misery they did. Those are just two reasons that Korean women might feel discouraged from wanting to have kids, and just throwing money at the situation without actually listening to women and trying to find a real solution isn't going to fix it. 

In many countries, there aren't enough social systems and support for women to make them feel safe and comfortable enough to start a family. Especially with the increase in far-right ideology among young men these days, women would rather be single than put up with what their grandmothers did since we don't need to rely on men to make a living anymore. But even among couples, with the economic and political turmoil happening in the world, many young people don't want to bring a child into the mix until they know for certain they can provide a safe and happy life for them. Furthermore, as it is becoming more socially acceptable to remain childless, more and more men and women are choosing not to have children simply because they don't want them. 

I'm really enjoying the video and find it fascinating , but I must say that simply blaming women's desire for status as the reason populations are decreasing worldwide isn't accurate.

### **@Shuffzord**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hE4l9WyLF3U&lc=UgwefLDA5AaO_6zjhC94AaABAg)

Great video, but I think the wealth → fertility decline thesis is missing a key variable: it's not about *how much* wealth, it's about *how it's distributed*.



Saudi Arabia is the obvious outlier - high national wealth, but fertility stayed elevated longer than the model predicts. Why? Rentier state dynamics, low female labor participation, strong religious norms. When wealth is passively transferred to citizens rather than competed for, the cost of having kids changes. That's a different game entirely.



Scandinavia flips it the other way - high wealth, high equality, generous parental support systems, and fertility is *still* below replacement. So distribution isn't the full answer either. Female autonomy and opportunity cost of childbearing seem to be doing a lot of independent work there.



The real insight I think is this: religion, status, and biology are probably the right top-level variables - but their *weight* is what actually drives outcomes, and that weight is socially determined and constantly shifting. These variables don't operate independently, they feed into each other. Religion shapes how status is defined. Status shapes biological timing. Biology shapes cultural norms around family. It's circular.



So my genuine question is: 
How do you even formalize correlations in a system this chaotic? When variables are hidden, interdependent, and context-specific, are standard causal inference tools actually solving the problem - or just giving us the most defensible narrative? Would love to hear if anyone has seen a framework that tackles the interdependency rather than just controlling for it.

I have a comment about the last part. As you can see in the table, there are countries within the replacement fertility range—perhaps around 2.0 or 1.85—that have an equal or even higher GDP. Therefore, your analysis of the table might collapse.
For example, if you look at a country with a 1.9 fertility rate and a population of 100 million, they would have roughly 1.9 million births per year. On the other hand, a country like Israel has only 9 million people; even if their fertility rate is over 3.0, they would only have about 270,000 births. So, I think your analysis of the table is completely wrong. There are also dozens of different factors that influence growth and the future.

### **@EvenTheDogAgrees**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hE4l9WyLF3U&lc=UgyViZ6L4zFf-NkWfUp4AaABAg)

I notice a lot of assumptions being accepted as truths without a good explanation or motivation. Some even that contradict the reality we see around us.

Some examples from the three stages of the lifecycle of societies:

1. Why would a low-tech, low wealth, low competition society be a free-for-all orgy? I know you explained it because women would mate with as many men as possible for protection, but that's not what we see in e.g. primitive tribal societies.
2. Why would a middle tech/wealth society necessarily be high competition? The level of competition is a function of geography, not wealth or technology. Although an argument could be made for the notion that a region that supports the accumulation of wealth and the sort of lifestyle that allows for sufficient time away from the struggle to survive that technological advancement becomes a possibility will attract competitors, it really does depend a lot on other factors, mostly geographical in nature. E.g. how accessible is this region from the outside world? How much better is this region compared to other, neighbouring regions? What's the population density over all different civilisations in the area? If my land is about as good as your land, and my land offers more resources than my people can sensibly consume, why would I risk the lives of my people and the continued existence of my civilisation attacking you in order to gain some land and resources I have no need of?
3. And why would the mid tech/wealth society necessarily default to arranged marriages? I history, we see quite the opposite: commoners were usually free to choose their partners, whereas the wealthy elites were forced into arranged marriages for political reasons. These two groups didn't really intermingle, and could be seen as two different "neighbouring" societies, where the commoners were the mid tech/wealth civ (at least compared to the barbarians), and the elites the high wealth and (relatively to the baseline for their world in their time period) high tech society.
4. Why does dating (aka courtship) only happen in high tech/wealth societies? Even primitive tribes practice courtship rituals.
5. Why would all women mating with the "top dogs" lead to society's downfall? That's exactly how things go in large parts of the animal kingdom, and it's never been a factor in the extinction of any species. Why would this lead to fewer children? A man can impregnate multiple women. You said it yourself: how many children does Elon Musk have? A woman, given access to an infinite number of men, under ideal conditions, can only give birth once every 9 months. Whereas a man, given access to an infinite number of women, under those same ideal conditions, can cause 273 women to give birth in the same amount of time. Assuming he limits himself to having sex with only one woman per day.

Or the whole bit about the male 4's and 5's getting all the women and the 1's through 3's becoming incels, whereas every woman gets to have intercourse with the 4's and 5's? In the real world, there are people that fall off the dating boat on both sides, male and female. While the male 4's and 5's have the option to have sex with the 5's down to the 1's, they usually have their standards and wouldn't be willing to go below a certain level, say a 3. After all, for a 4 or 5, there is no status to be gained from having sex with a 2 or a 1. Quite the contrary, the 2 or 1 is "what a 3 or lower has to settle for because unlike me, they can't score with a 4 or 5". So being seen with them would lead to a _loss_ in status, similar to a millionaire driving a Kia. People generally date within their own attractiveness range, plus or minus a margin. Even the 1's are dating other 1's or 2's. Although I'll grant you that they do have more difficulty finding mates. But that goes for both genders, not just males, and is the result of modern media having conditioned us all to expect at least a certain level of attractiveness in a potential partner. In movies every princess ends up with a handsome prince, and the boy always gets the attractive girl. So a lot of 1's and 2's consistently aim too high, fail to learn from the repeated rejections, and either give up in despair or find themselves past their prime dating years without a mate because they wouldn't "settle for less".

Now, I'm sure this _actual_ reality could also be perfectly explained using game theory. But the fact that the same theory can be used to explain both reality and a contradictory non-reality does raise some questions. Namely: is the theory just wrong? If it can be contorted to explain literally any scenario, real or imagined, it's not much of a theory, is it? Or does the issue lie with the understanding of the presenter? Or is it merely their ability to properly explain the theory that's the issue here?

Overall, I was expecting a bit more from this presentation.

I think this view is too "macro," especially for Israel. While the country does have a high fertility rate, a closer analysis reveals that this is primarily due to a poorly educated and underemployed group. Within this group, only women work, and they hold low-skilled jobs; the men do not work and must dedicate their days to religious study. As a result, these families are poor and heavily dependent on social assistance. Furthermore, they are extremely religious. These are the ultra-Orthodox Jews, who have an average of four to six children, or even more, per woman.



This group represents nearly 30% of the Israeli population, and this figure is increasing year after year.



Thus, given these criteria for Israel, this means that the human, innovative, and economic capital of the population is being diluted year after year within this mass of ultra-Orthodox Jews. Israel is therefore wealthy and has a large population, certainly, but it is losing its skilled human capital. And the situation is not improving because these constraints (a working woman, a poorly educated man forced to practice his religion all day) are an integral part of their faith and are non-negotiable for them.



Religion is important and influences demographics, but if it hinders education and employment, it is not enough.



Islam, on the other hand, does not impose the same limitations as the religion of ultra-Orthodox Jews.
